The DMA-on word is written on every row tick; the effects are arpeggio, slides, tone portamento, vibrato, volume slide, set volume, jump, pattern break and speed. Recognition is by content (the 31 sample headers, the four counters and the pointer table, as the specification gives them; no name check is needed) | Peter Verswyvelen Packer (the 68k player UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | 12 of 12 modules recognised as `Peter Verswyvelen`. The channels are visited from the last to the first; every row writes every channel's period and volume, a note start writes the sample before the DMA is switched off and on again at the end of the row, and every tick rewrites the loop registers and the DMA-on word. Effects: sample offset, set volume, jump, break, speed, fine slides and volume, and per tick arpeggio, slide, tone portamento, vibrato and the volume-slide combinations. The samples are in `smp.NAME` beside `alp.NAME` (read with the song file's name, in any case) or follow the song data in the same file; a module whose companion is missing is not played. Recognition is by content (the four magic bytes, a positive size, the parts fitting in the file). | Maniacs of Noise (`mon.*`; Flimbo's Quest, Battle Chess II, Dragon Wars, Lord of the Rings, Mysterious Worlds, Unreal, Rubicon, the Habitual Novelty demo) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/mon.rs`). A module is a raw 68000 image carrying the game's own replay routine, which is not run: the song, instrument and period tables are located in it by a few short structural signatures of the code, and the behaviour of its builds is written from the specification (build 1, build 2 with 16-byte song entries and wave-table instruments, build 3a/3b/3c with 12-byte entries, ten pattern commands, sustain envelopes, a pitch envelope and voice enable mask in 3b, wave-sequence instruments in 3c). Four voices, one Paula channel each, 50 Hz, the duration multiplier sets the tempo; order list, repeat counts, fades and end-of-music. Takes the files whose three entry words are `JMP d16(PC)` and whose init routine begins `LEA d16(PC),A5` / `BCLR #0,d16(A5)` (the 34 corpus files: `mon.*` of the archive and the `.mon` files of the demo scene); open replayers are tried first, so this port plays them and the M.O.N. DeliPlayer is not used for them when `--open-replayers` is on. Not the `MON_Old_Version` files, which start with an `andi.b` and belong to the Jeroen Tel player | Maniacs of Noise (the 68k player UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | 91 of 91 runs (every subsong of the 34 files) give the same ordered Paula writes as UADE for 20 s (the audio registers and DMACON from the first tick on, sample addresses compared relative to each run's first one, times not compared), and the song-end tick of the 30 runs that end (end-of-music command or completed fade-out) is UADE's last tick in every one. No module of the 7,546 present of the corpus lists' 10,181 listed modules is claimed. No regression: 3 modules of each `ports/*.txt` list give identical WAV hashes before and after |
| Special FX (`jd.*`; Batman, Rambo III, RoboCop 2, Elf, Head Over Heels, Hudson Hawk, Midnight Resistance, Navy SEALs, The New Zealand Story, Operation Thunderbolt, Striker Rage, Wild Wheels, Battle Command; Jonathan Dunn, Matthew Cannon, Keith Tinman, 1989-93) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/specialfx.rs`). A file is the memory image of a whole 68000 program (branch table, play routine, tables, 8SVX samples); the code is not run. It is read only to find the data, by generic instruction anchors: the branch table, the period-table load of the play routine (which gives the load address), the four table loads of the voice routine (pattern, pitch, envelope and instrument tables, each self- or base-relative), the song table and subsong count in the start routine, the shift counts and volume writes of the hardware routine, the leading loads and calls of the instrument-building routine (four list encodings) and the load before `MOVEQ #2,D1` (the silence buffer). The engine is written from its behaviour: four voices (three in The New Zealand Story), 50 Hz, order lists of patterns, notes with per-note envelopes of volume ramps and a release marker, pitch tables, vibrato, slide and "drop" effects, rests, fixed durations, the instruments built once from the 8SVX chunks (one-shot part then the repeat part). The song ends when every voice of the song has jumped to its order-list loop point once. Recognition is by content: the exact branch-table test (four or seven branches and the two address loads of the start routine). Departures from the specification, from the black-box runs: the silence buffer of the New Zealand Story list encoding is written 2 bytes below its load operand; the words before the period table (read by a note below the table's range, Head Over Heels song 6) are the code's 32-bit absolute operands relocated to the reference host's load address 0xF000, which is how the original run sees them (an operand is taken to be a word after an instruction with an absolute-long operand and a high half below 0x100000), and the instrument records are read with the same address, so out-of-range period indexes, whose result depends on the load address of the original run, play what the reference's run played; the unguarded duration decrement of the builds with a four-entry branch table is applied to that layout only (unexercised) | Special FX (the 68k player UADE (`uade123`) ships, `jd.*`), run with its write log | 29 of 29 corpus modules recognised as `Special FX`. Ordered Paula writes against UADE's, tick by tick, 20 s: 359 of 359 subsong runs identical (53 reach 20 s, 306 end earlier; the audio registers and DMACON from the first volume write on, sample addresses compared relative to the load address, times not compared; UADE's host stop at the end of a run is not compared), and in the 306 early-ending runs the port's song-end tick is UADE's last tick in every one. 200 s with UADE's song-end detection off (subsongs 1 and 2 of every module): 49 of 49 identical (22 run the full 200 s; in the 27 others UADE stops the run itself and the port's writes up to that point are identical, and silent afterwards). Two of the 7,546 present of the corpus lists' 10,181 listed modules are claimed, `jd.sfx` of Battle Command and of RoboCop 2 (both are Special FX modules of the corpus, in `soundfx.txt`); nothing else. No regression: 3 modules of each `ports/*.txt` list give identical WAV hashes before and after |
| Jason Page (`jpn.*` with a sample companion `smp.*`; Realms, Fire & Ice, Uridium 2, Empire Soccer 94, Xmas demo) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/jasonpage.rs`). The Graftgold music system: pure data, a song file and a sample file (raw signed 8-bit PCM, one block that two sound commands also rewrite while it plays). A 50 Hz pattern engine, divided by the song's speed, feeds four voices from order lists of patterns; a note asks for a sound (by priority) and a sound is a small command stream (sample, length, loop, wait, vibrato, envelope, DMA, pitch, sample copy and morph). Two layouts are written: Fire & Ice, Uridium 2, Empire Soccer 94 and the Xmas demo (16-bit offsets), and Realms (32-bit offsets, pre-multiplied opcodes, its long tune cut into 16 subsongs); the third layout (ViroCop AGA) is in no corpus and declined. Recognition is by content (the header's table offsets, or the two magic longs after Realms' pattern pointers). It takes `jpn.*` files whose companion is found, and is tried before the Eagleplayer "Jason Page New", which therefore plays only the files this one declines (no companion, or the third layout). A song end (the end marker of any voice) restarts the song, as the original does | Jason Page (the 68k player UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | 79 of 79 runs (every subsong of the 16 distinct modules and the two CD32 duplicates: 60 of the first layout, 19 of Realms) give the same ordered Paula writes as UADE for 20 s (the audio registers and DMACON from the first tick on, sample addresses compared relative to each run's first one, times not compared), including the 29 runs in which the end marker restarts the song one to 999 times (checked with the song-end stop of the host switched off; with it on, the port stops at the first end). Recognition is by content (the Eagleplayer's test: the first driver entry, the branch to the expected instruction), plus the layout and preparation succeeding. Decisions where the specification is silent, from the black-box runs: the image is prepared for the reference host's address 0xF000 and converted to the guest address only for Paula's location registers, so the one read past the period table (Alien Storm song 5, which reads a converted song-table pointer) plays what the reference played; the voice records' other fields start at zero (their image values are not read); word-format track elements are compared as whole words; the start-up writes (the volume set-up and the song start) come in the first tick, before its play routine; the waits between writes are the reference's medians (Die Hard 2 song 1), so the DMA off-to-on gaps match it | Mike Davies (the 68k player UADE (`uade123`) ships, the Wanted Team Eagleplayer), run with its write log | 9 of 9 corpus modules recognised as `Mike Davies`. The reference runs play on past the song end; the port's song-end tick is UADE's own (checked with `uade123 -1` for 8 songs: Mercs 3 and 6, Die Hard 2 5, Alien Storm 7, Strider 4, Dynasty Wars 3, Italy 1990 2, Indiana Jones 3 2). The DMA off-to-on gaps: median 2,712 colour clocks against the reference's 2,686. None of the 7,546 present of the corpus lists' 10,181 listed modules is claimed. No regression: 3 modules of each `ports/*.txt` list give identical WAV hashes before and after |

The song file (`OBISYNTHPACK`) holds 64 positions (rows, track set, repeat count or command, voice mask), 256 track sets of four slot entries (pattern, instrument offset, transposition, speed) and the patterns, lists of six-byte events at their rows; the instruments, synthetic waveforms and samples with an ADSR envelope, tremolo, vibrato, pulse-width modulation (a window moved over a two-bank waveform, its LC written every tick) and portamento, are in the companion file. Subsongs start at the positions whose mask has any of bits 4-7, counted as the Eagleplayer counts them (3 for `animus`, 6 for `main`). The song end is announced at the commands FF (the position repeats), FE (all voices released, the sequencer stops) and FC (the jump back to the loop mark); none stops the song. The companion is `SMP.NAME` for `OSP.NAME` (any case), else `SMP.set`; one that does not start with `OBISYNTHPACK` is refused. Recognition is by content (`OBISYNTHPACK` and the word 0x0100 at 0x50C; `smp.set` has another word there and is refused). The later format version (a zero long at 0x50C) is not played: no file uses it. The original's time between its writes is waited (about 180, 450 and 520 colour clocks) so a started sample's start is fetched before its loop is queued | Synth Pack (the 68k player UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | 24 of 24 runs (every subsong of the 9 corpus songs for 20 s, and 8 runs of 15 to 135 s past the song end, the loop and the stop commands) give the same Paula writes as UADE tick for tick (the audio registers and DMACON in order in every tick, and the start-up writes; sample addresses bound to one base for the whole run, times not compared); the song end is announced in the tick of the FE, FF or FC command (e.g. `lost` at tick 4820, its FE stop). | Cinemaware (`cin.*` with its 8SVX samples in an `Instruments` folder beside it; Wings) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/cinemaware.rs`, with the 240-step pitch table written out from the specification's table). The module (`IBLK`) is pure data: instrument records selected by MIDI program number, each with up to three key-ranged waves (a rate, start, loop and end), an 8-step volume envelope and pitch envelope with sustain and release indices, an LFO and a pitch-bend range; a table giving each of the 16 MIDI channels a fixed Paula voice or dynamic allocation (a score over free, released, same-channel, same-note and oldest voices); after `ASEQ` a time-stamped list of MIDI-style events (note on and off, volume, sustain pedal and modulation controllers, program change, pitch bend, end). Every wave slot loads its own copy of its file (`Instruments/NAME`, any case, also from in-memory files), its data 8 bytes after the first `BODY`; a missing file refuses the module. A note mutes its voice (period 0x80, DMA off) and sets its start, the voice's DMA goes on in the same tick and its loop (or a silent buffer) is queued two ticks later; the period is `rate << 20` over a pitch divider of the note, transposition, LFO, pitch envelope and bend in 1/20 semitones. The tick is the game's 60 Hz CIA timer (11822 E-clock cycles); at the end row the song end is announced, 25 ticks only turn DMA off, and the song starts again. Recognition is by content (`IBLK`, 1-128 records, `ASEQ` at an even distance up to 254 bytes after the channel table). The original's time between its writes is waited (about 1,100 to 1,900 colour clocks) so a muted voice stops before its DMA goes on again | Cinemaware (the 68k player UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | 14 of 14 runs (the 11 corpus files for 20 s, and 3 of them for 60 s through the song end, the 25-tick pause and the restart) give the same Paula writes as UADE tick for tick (the audio registers and DMACON in order in every tick, and the start-up writes; sample addresses bound one-to-one to UADE's for the whole run, times not compared); the song end comes at the corpus's song lengths (1505, 1384, 1132 and 1859 ticks for `ingame_05`, `06`, `08`, `10`). Audio against amigaplayer-rs running UADE's Cinemaware Eagleplayer: median spectral distance 2.1 dB (`ingame_09`) and 3.7 dB (`ingame_04`). No module of the 7,546 present of the corpus lists' 10,181 listed modules is claimed. No regression: 3 modules of each `ports/*.txt` list give identical WAV hashes before and after |
| Jason Brooke (`jcb.*`; 1943, After Burner '89, Feud, Harley-Davidson, Heavy Metal, Hellfire Attack, Ikari Warriors, Out Run, Pac-Land, Resolution 101, Rockford, Side Arms, Space Harrier, Thunder Blade, Thunderstrike, Vixen, World Darts; US Gold / Tiertex, 1987-90) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/jasonbrooke.rs`). A `jcb` file is a raw memory image of the game's sound driver (68k code, variables, tables, songs, samples); the code is not run. It is read only to find the tables and variables, by the PC-relative operands of a few generic instructions after the play routine (periods, commands, instruments, silent sample, envelopes and their index and start levels, arpeggios and their start table, pulse-width buffer and its length word, record stride, note threshold, DMA-state field, global transpose, the start routine's clears and song table), and to tell the builds apart: B (record stride 0x28 or 0x34), B2 (stride 0x2C: other record layout, pattern coding, arpeggio mode, repeat commands; command 4C's field and commands 55/56 read from the first instruction of their handlers) and A (Feud, Pac-Land, World Darts: a waveform synthesiser, its variants by the presence of a few instructions). The engine is written from their behaviour: four channel records kept in a copy of the image (the drivers read some fields past their records), 50 Hz, frame skip, tempo accumulator, a pulse-width generator writing into the playing sample, two-step sample starts, envelopes, arpeggios, vibrato, slides, bends, transpose. For build B and B2 the Eagleplayer's JSR/NOP rewrites of ten instruction sequences are applied to the copy, as the specification gives them, because notes read periods past the table onto them (Space Harrier song 3, Thunderstrike song 0); the stub address is the host's and reads as 0 (no corpus note reads it). Pac-Land: its envelope index uses the high byte of a register the host leaves behind; the port takes 0 at the start of each tick, which is what the engine's host leaves there at its fixed start phase (the reference run's envelopes then play; UADE's own score left INTENAR, 0x40, whose reads fell past the image, so under uade123 the song is nearly silent); another host would leave another value. Song end at the end command, or when every channel has wrapped its sequence list as the Eagleplayer tests it (a byte 0x32 into the record) | JasonBrooke (the 68k player UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | 20 of 20 corpus modules recognised as `Jason Brooke`. Ordered Paula writes (DMACON, ADKCON, the module's INTENA writes, AUDxLC/LEN/PER/VOL; sample addresses relative to the run's first one, times not compared) against UADE's, tick by tick, start-up writes included: 148 of 149 runs identical: B-28 and B-34 60/60 (every subsong for 20 s) and 60/60 (120 s; the 14 that UADE stops early, 20 s after the end command's silence, are identical to their end), B2 11/11 and 4/4 (120 s), A 13/14. The 149th, Pac-Land song 6, is identical up to its end command, which it reaches silent after 1.9 s, so the engine's empty-subsong skip goes on to song 7 where UADE (run with `-n`) plays on; with the song end not reported it is identical for all 20 s. None of the 7,546 present of the corpus lists' 10,181 listed modules is claimed. No regression: 3 modules of each `ports/*.txt` list give identical WAV hashes before and after |
| Sound Images (`tw.*`, Tony Williams' driver; Shinobi, Chuckie Egg II, Judge Dredd, Never Mind, Nitro, Narc, Smash TV, International Ice Hockey, Super Monaco GP, Teenage Mutant Hero Turtles, Viz, Future Basketball, MoonFall, Sharkey's Moll; 1989-92) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/soundimages.rs`). A `tw` file is a flat 68k image of the game's replay driver, its state blocks, tables, songs and samples; the code is not run. Its tables (voice blocks, song base and stride, sample, arpeggio, period and volume tables, command jump table) are found as the targets of the PC-relative operands of a few generic instructions, nine driver revisions are told apart by a fingerprint (voice-block count, volume table, dispatcher range bounds, master-volume offset, the active-byte test), and the engine is written from their behaviour: three or four voices, an order list of byte streams each (notes, then ranges for step length, sample, envelope, start volume and arpeggio, and a jump table of 17 to 24 commands: next pattern, jump, stop, release, rest, transposition, vibrato, portamento, slides, tie, volume steps, fade), a tempo counter, attack/decay/release envelopes on timers, the loop pointer installed from the audio interrupt, and the two older revisions' generated pulse buffers. The voice blocks keep the file's layout and initial values (Judge Dredd's never-set sample start of 0, reads past the period and volume tables). The two variable jump-table entries are classified relative to their neighbours (a handler beginning with slide-down's instruction is slide-up, one with tie-off's operand tie-on, else volume-down), which gives the specification's four groups. Recognition is by content (the start branch, the play routine's register save and custom-chip base, the voice blocks' Paula addresses and DMA words, a song base and at least one subsong); it takes the 29 `tw` files and nothing else: the Dave Lowe New layout-A files, which the Eagleplayer 2.06 "Sound Images" player also accepts, start with a header word (4 or 8), not the branch, and stay with `Dave Lowe New`. Song end as UADE has it (the song stopped, or every voice with a header read its order list's terminator). Departures, decided from the black-box runs: the audio interrupt is taken at the DMA start, as Paula raises it (UADE loses a race in some ticks and takes it at the end of the first buffer, so the attack plays twice there); Super Monaco GP in-game holds stale pending DMA and interrupt words that crash UADE's run after one tick, so the port clears them at load and plays the music (checked against UADE runs of the file with the two words zeroed); Never Mind's volumes are UADE's (its patch writes them x 4); Never Mind writes its periods after a stop in the same tick; commands past a revision's map (none in the corpus) are skipped | Sound Images (the 68k player UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | 29 of 29 modules recognised as `Sound Images`. Ordered Paula writes against UADE's, tick by tick, 20 s or to the song end: 122 of the 128 module and subsong runs identical (A 22/22, A9C 1/1, B 47/53, R1 10/10, R2 6/6, R3 18/18, R4 8/8, R5 4/4, R6 6/6; DMACON values in full, sample addresses relative to the load address, the audio interrupt's loop-pointer writes checked apart: each of UADE's 13,395 is the port's for the same DMA start, 288 of them one or more ticks later); the 6 others are the Super Monaco GP in-game one-tick crash runs, and against UADE runs of that file with the stale words zeroed all 6 are identical. Shinobi subsong 5's tick 601, a Paula-emulation artefact, is not special-cased. In the 59 runs that end before 20 s (and the 3 of the zeroed Super Monaco GP runs) the song-end tick is UADE's. Of the 7,546 present of the corpus lists' 10,181 listed modules only 3 are claimed, corpus files that `soundfx.txt` lists (`tw.sfx` of three games); none of the 41 Dave Lowe New files is. No regression: 3 modules of each `ports/*.txt` list give identical WAV hashes before and after |

| Sound Master (`sm.*`; Doofus, Future Shock) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/soundmaster.rs`). The files are raw memory images of Michiel Soede's replay routine compiled together with one song and its samples; the routine is not run. Its variable base is located from the init routine's first PC-relative LEA and the period table (read from the module) from the LEA after the note masking; the three builds of the corpus (A: Doofus, its tables behind an offset table and a master volume per voice; B: Future Shock; C: an unused Doofus song, B's layout shifted by 16 bytes, with a sample sweep and the A period rule) are written from their behaviour: four voices, one Paula channel each, 50 Hz, a step table of pattern-list ranges with a transposition, an instrument offset per voice and a fade byte, patterns of (note, parameter) rows (notes, ties, portamento, hold, volume, speed and break commands), 64 instruments with an attack/release envelope, a vibrato with a depth increment (and in B a volume vibrato) and an arpeggio of notes and samples, the Paula loop registers re-written every tick. Recognition is by content: the three branches, the init routine's LEA and the audio-filter operand before its RTS, the build's signature words; an unknown build is refused. The song wraps from its end step to its loop step, where the song end is announced and play goes on (UADE plays on there). Departures from the specification, from the black-box runs: the effect pass writes the period (`cur >> 2`) every tick of every voice whose arpeggio does not set it, also with the vibrato off or delayed (the specification lists the write with the vibrato); the sweep counter counts every tick, and the sweep step applies when its masked bits are 0; build C's sweep test reads the last arpeggio value of the tick, 0xFF after a step load (not observable in the corpus); build A's sound-effect entry points are not offered (the host never calls them), its play mode is the Eagleplayer's fixed 31 | Sound Master (the 68k player UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | 16 of 16 runs (the 13 files for 20 s, and 110, 140 and 280 s runs of one file of each build, through the song wrap) give the same ordered Paula writes as UADE in every tick (the audio registers and DMACON from the module's init on, sample addresses compared relative to each run's first one, AUDxDAT writes and times not compared). The song end comes at the wrap ticks of the long runs (5119, 6719, 13535) and at the specification's predicted ones for the other files. | Tim Follin II (`tf.*`; Ghouls'n'Ghosts, Sly Spy - Secret Agent, Puzznic; the driver Tim Follin wrote with Mike Delaney) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/timfollin.rs`). A file is a raw memory dump of the game's music data (song table, instrument tables, song types, byte-coded sequences, instrument records with their samples) with absolute pointers to the game's load address; the replay routine is not in the file and is written from its behaviour: four voices, one Paula channel each, 50 Hz, notes and 19 commands (instrument, call / return / jump, repeat, envelope, glide, trill, vibrato, transposition, fixed duration, a retrigger of a second instrument one tick after a note, DMA off before a note's end, pulse-width modulation, end of voice). The pulse-width modulation rewrites bytes of the sample Paula plays, so the port writes them into the module in guest memory (Paula run up to the moment of each store). The call stacks, song number, fade byte, DMA masks, register addresses and period table are kept in one work area with the original's layout, so the unchecked stacks spill as they did (Puzznic song 5 after six minutes: the song number, then the fade byte). Recognition is by content: the jump-table build (Puzznic: six `jmp` entries and the entry-address difference of 0xA0) or the program-header build (Ghouls'n'Ghosts, Sly Spy: `601A`, the word `1010`/`1012`, song 0's four voices on one sequence and that sequence found). The shipping build's Eagleplayer/DeliPlayer "Tim Follin II" also plays these files; open replayers are tried first, so this port takes all `tf.*` files of both builds when `--open-replayers` is on. Subsongs as UADE offers them: 1..8 for Puzznic (song k plays song k of the table), 1 for the header build. Decisions where the specification leaves a choice: positions are kept for the reference host's load address 0x10000, so the spill writes the same values; the song ends when every voice has ended (the original never reports an end and stays silent) or when a spilled fade runs out (the original crashes there); the period table's words beyond entry 96 (the original's own memory) read as 0, and the second relocation list, which the replay never reads, is not relocated; the writes are spread over the tick by rough medians of the reference's gaps | Tim Follin II (the 68k player UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | 22 of 22 corpus files recognised as `Tim Follin II` (the 13 archive files and 9 derived Puzznic files with song 2..10 in song 1's place). Ordered Paula writes against UADE's: 92 of 92 runs identical for 20 s (every subsong UADE offers of every file; the audio registers and DMACON from UADE's DMA enable on, sample addresses relative to each run's first one, times not compared); the 450 s run of Puzznic song 5 identical to the fade's expiry at tick 21571 (UADE's two crash writes after it not compared). Every audio DMA fetch word inside the module of the 92 runs (35.9 M, 345 k of them of rewritten sample words) is the port's memory before or after the fetch's tick. Song-end ticks where every voice ends: Ghouls'n'Ghosts COUNT 588, HIGH 5113, Puzznic songs 2 and 3 215 and 338, 7 642, song 9 0, the same ticks at which UADE's writes stop. | Digital Sonix & Chrome (`dsc.*`; Dragon's Breath, Andrew E. Bailey and David M. Hanlon, 1990) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/dsc.rs`). A pure-data sample sequencer: a song table of blocks of rows (offset, repeat count, rows; a zero repeat count ends a subsong), four voice tracks of one byte a row (a sample number or 0xFF), 18-byte sample records with a repeated loop part, one row every `(W/2 + 1500)/W` ticks of 50 Hz. The tick only starts samples (a fixed write sequence through manual mode: DMA off, period 1, interrupt off, AUDxDAT 0, then a wait for the channel's request before the period and DMA on); four audio-interrupt handlers (`Replayer::audio_irq_ch`) chain the parts: at the start of each DMA block the loop part, as many times as the record says, then a 2-word silent buffer the port allocates, then the interrupt off. The original leaves two races with Paula to the machine's timing (a first interrupt lost between DMA on and the clearing of the request, so the whole sample plays twice; a handler run inside the trigger). The port decides them as the specification says a host must: it makes the writes in the original's order with the gaps measured in the reference runs (about 371 colour clocks from the tick to the first voice, 58 a voice, the trigger's own gaps, 56 more before the poll when the record has repeats, a 14-clock poll), lets the emulated Paula raise its interrupts on the way and runs the handlers when they are raised, also inside the tick, each writing at the raster position the original waits for (the next line, 66-77 clocks into it). Recognition is the Eagleplayer's test as the specification gives it (header ranges, the song table and its terminator, every sample record inside the sample data, the last one ending it) | Digital Sonix & Chrome (the 68k player UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | 19 runs (every subsong of the 12 corpus files, 20 s): the per-channel order of writes is the same as UADE's for 37 of the 76 channel runs, 18,501 of 18,981 ticks have the same ordered Paula writes (the audio registers incl. AUDxDAT, DMACON, the audio bits of INTENA/INTREQ; sample addresses relative to each run's first one, the silent buffer by its role, times not compared), and 1,345 of 1,513 note triggers give the same writes up to the channel's next trigger, tick by tick; no run is identical throughout, because the races fall where the host's raster phase puts them: the tick of this host runs at another phase of the raster than in each UADE run (which differs from run to run), so the first interrupt is lost for 68 of the 1,577 triggers (UADE: 47; 2 of them the same ones), and the same race outcome as UADE's comes for 1,466 of the 1,577 (never losing it would give 1,530). With the tick moved to each UADE run's raster phase (an experiment, not shipped) 1,371 of 1,513 triggers match, and 23 of the 47 lost interrupts are lost in the port too (the reference's 68020 jitters by 1-2 clocks within an 8-clock window). The song end comes at UADE's time in all 10 runs where UADE's player reports one within 130 s (1.5 s to 102.3 s); fanfares subsong 2 ends at 38.3 s, after UADE's silence detector has stopped it. No module of the 7,546 present of the corpus lists' 10,181 listed modules is claimed. No regression: 3 modules of each `ports/*.txt` list give identical WAV hashes before and after |

Hanlon: Populous, Fusion, Enlightenment; DTRK songs with their kit and `smp.*` 8SVX bank beside them; not Digital Sonix & Chrome) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification (`docs/specs/digitalsoundcreations/SPEC.md`) and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/digitalsoundcreations.rs`). The port keeps the requests as the engine's audio.device does and writes what it writes when it would (sample and DMA at the request, period and volume when the engine sees the channel's interrupt: the port is called after each output sample and vertical blank through `next_tick_at`). DTRK period = 3579545 DIVU the rate's low word (overflow keeps the dividend's low word); tick `5 * (7499855 / (T + 30) - 176)` colour clocks for the header tempo T; Populous 50 Hz. Times within a tick: a 68000-cycle model of the reference's code between its host calls, exact over all 42,112 call gaps of the corpus. Decisions: pattern numbers above 63 play empty rows, rate 0 (the original's division by zero) takes the overflow period, a gap in the bank is one empty sample slot (seen once), loop-request timing on tracks 2 and 3 extrapolated | `Digital Sound Creations` (Eagleplayer, Wanted Team) on the 68000 engine, with its write log and OS trace; not in EP 2.06 nor UADE | All 6 songs recognised; none of the 8,652 readable files of the `ports/*.txt` lists is taken. The song end is reported in the tick in which the reference restarts its stream: tick for tick in every identical run that passes it, and within the 20 ms step at which the check samples it for Colorado subsongs 2-5, Starblade 1, Wind Surf Willy 1 and Boston Bomb Club (UADE without `-n` stops 0 to 3 ticks after it). The handler is served from the host's Paula emulation and is deferred as the original's is: the replayer keeps the pending requests, and a handler that is due at the tick runs before it. When the main-context writes happen within a tick decides in which tick a one-shot's end is served. Their moments are therefore per-event costs, fitted to the reference logs per build family. The song ends when every voice has ended, at the all-voices loop mark, or at TMNT's stop command, and is reported in the tick that signals it | Kris Hatlelid (the Wanted Team Eagleplayer UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | 13 of 13 corpus modules recognised as `Kris Hatlelid`; the 7 `songplay` companions are not claimed. Then the port serves a one-shot end or loop on channel 3 one tick before the reference, where that interrupt falls close to the tick boundary and the reference's own interrupt and raster timing decide it. The song end is at UADE's tick in 64 of 71 subsongs. In the other 7 it is one tick before UADE's, which the specification documents as a host artefact. A file is an AmigaDOS load file of one code hunk with the `J.FLOGEL` signature and the init, play and subsong-count fields at its head; every module carries its own replayer, which is never run. Its constants are read from its instructions structurally: the gate word (a `tst.w` at the top of the play routine), the step length (the immediate that reloads the step counter), the three channels' mode jump tables (nine `bra.w` each) with the shape of each mode's handler (the sample pointer and length written, the period routine called, the shift of the period, the DMA word on or off), the period routine's table and note offset (an immediate or a memory word, at its width), the subsong start triples of the init routine and the restart block, whose Paula writes are taken in order (the pointer written to a pointer or data slot, the length, the raw volumes, the DMA and interrupt-enable words, Luxor's order included). The engine is written from the behaviour: a step every N ticks; three channels with a duration, a mode and a stream pointer; `FF` restarts (the first one of a run is the song end), `FE x` sets the mode, any other byte is a duration followed by what the mode reads; the host's volume 56 after each period. The write moments are fitted to the reference: the block timings and the 32-clock `FE` step; the Eagleplayer's level-3 frame interrupt (every 70,824 colour clocks), whose 66-clock handler preempts the CIA tick (every 70,940) when the drift between them carries it into a burst, which is where the reference's bursts across a frame boundary come from. Decisions where the specification leaves the choice: the first tick of a run is the init and the first step the next; a mode outside the jump table reads and writes nothing; reads outside the image give 0 and the period table is read from the image; the shift is to the left in 16 bits; the write moments of modes 2 to 6, which the corpus never uses, are by analogy | Janko Mrsic-Flogel (the Wanted Team Eagleplayer UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | 6 of 6 corpus modules recognised as `Janko Mrsic-Flogel`. Ordered Paula writes against UADE's, tick by tick (the audio registers, DMACON and INTENA clears from the start-up on, sample addresses relative to the run's first, UADE's host stop dropped): 32 of 32 runs identical (every subsong of the 6 files, with the song-end stop and past it; Bomb Jack subsong 0 past its song end only when the engine's own skip of an empty subsong, which UADE also makes, is switched off). The writes' moments against the reference's frame anchor (the CIA tick's acknowledge, the frame interrupt's phase set per run by `PK_SKEW`): all 23,188 writes of the 32 runs (the 12 to 14 start-up writes of each included, as intervals) at the reference's moment to the colour clock. None of the 7,546 present of the corpus lists' 10,181 listed modules is claimed. No regression: 3 modules of each `ports/*.txt` list give identical WAV hashes before and after |
| Paul Robotham (`dat.*` with the sample companion `mdtest.ssd`, or `NAME.SSD` for `NAME.dat`; 1990s game music) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/paulrobotham.rs`; the period table is written out from the specification's). Four channels read a byte stream of notes, ties and commands (loops, vibrato, slide, envelope and sample selects, tempo, global volume slide, decay), with envelope tables and a 50 Hz tick; samples are offsets into the companion, which is read from the same directory (a module without it is refused). A note stops the DMA, sets address, length and period, the next tick starts the DMA, the one after installs the loop. The specification's "silent note" is not what the reference does (a sample selected in the no-retrigger mode makes a normal note), and the song restart keeps the periods, restart values, tempo and pending DMA bits, as the reference does. The song ends when channel 0 reaches the byte before channel 1's start | Paul Robotham (the Wanted Team Eagleplayer UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | All 3 corpus modules recognised as `Paul Robotham`. Ordered Paula writes (DMACON, AUDxLC/LEN/PER/VOL) compared against UADE's tick by tick: 12 of 12 20 s runs identical, and the full-length run of `dat.win` (16,499 ticks, through the song end at tick 12,668 and the restart) identical; the write moments differ by up to about 2,200 colour clocks. Times per write against the first write of its tick: per run 36 to 94% of the writes exact; the rest are early or late by up to about 430 colour clocks, and the DMACON write of some ticks up to 3,400 early (path-dependent costs of the original, not modelled). Build C restarts channel 3 only when the engine's Paula has raised its interrupt bit (`INTREQR` bit 10, acknowledged by the restart), writes channel 3 as a silent timer every frame but the first, always plays channel 1's table sample and writes its output also when stopped; channel 1 plays the module's own bytes at offset 52, not the host's patched image (audio only). Decisions where the specification leaves the choice: the first play call is held back 1,260 colour clocks after the init so that channel 3's blocks fall against the frames as in the reference (any value from 1,200 to 1,320 reproduces all runs; 1,380 and 1,438 fail at frame 7104 and 6838 of the 150 s run); the longest track list is taken over all subsongs, the later on a tie; the row countdown is decremented at each row step before it is tested; an envelope select or period index past its table reads whatever the module holds there, and the unexercised commands 146 to 183 do nothing; the voice-1 sweep and the voice-2 effect tail are written from the specification but nothing starts them (no reference run does); the write order inside the voice-2 output (address, period, length) follows the reference logs where the specification lists the length before the period | Rob Hubbard ST (the Eagleplayer UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | 3 of 3 modules recognised as `Rob Hubbard ST`. Ordered Paula writes (DMACON, ADKCON, module INTREQ acknowledges and AUDxLC/LEN/PER/VOL, start-up included, LC module-relative) against UADE's: 15 of 15 runs identical in order and value (Jupiter Probe: 20 s with and without `-n`, two reruns and the 150 s runs, 20,115 frames with 1,045 channel-3 restarts in the no-song-end run, the song ending at frame 5632; and Battleships subsong 0 and 1, each with and without `-n`, and the 40 s run of subsong 0; Gold Runner with and without `-n` and a rerun), 8,082 frames; the song ends where UADE stops (command at frame 288 of subsong 1, track wrap at frame 1792 of the 40 s run). Times per write against the frame anchor: 28 to 51% of the writes exact, 71 to 97% within 16 colour clocks, the largest difference 126 (the original's 68000 time per tick is modelled by event counts fitted to the references, not by cycles). None of the 7,546 present of the corpus lists' 10,181 listed modules is claimed. No regression: 3 modules of each `ports/*.txt` list (164 files) give identical WAV hashes before and after |
| Steve Barrett (`sb.*`; Kelly X, MUNGOMUSIC, Artificial Dreams, Advanced Ski Simulator, scape, and the Wally Beben build of Street Fighter; 6 modules, 16 subsongs) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/stevebarrett.rs`). A raw memory image (four branches: sample init, song init, play, stop; the code, its variables and tables, 8SVX samples). The code is not run: the entries, the stop routine's AUD0VOL load, the first PC-relative address load (the first sample) and the one before it (the pointer base) are matched on decoded instructions (`m68kdec`), and the layout (all 38 variable and table offsets, the pointer base, the subsong count) and the build flags (family, sample-init loops, subsong limit, repeat stage, portamento, instrument word swap, host wait) are derived from the module's own code by the specification's structural locator rules (`stevebarrett_loc.rs`: routines found by their content, variables by how the routines use them, cross-checks, data checks); no table keyed on the entries remains, so moved code, moved or re-ordered tables and exchanged variables are found, and a file for which any rule or check fails is refused. Still checked constants (spec L12): the voice-record field layout and the record sizes, and the 100 Hz rule (samples size 62372). Two code families (early: no slide, arpeggio or portamento; full) and the build differences (sample loops, subsong limit, repeat stage, portamento, swapped instrument words), the player's state kept in a working copy of the module as the specification requires, 50 Hz (100 Hz for the one build with the 62372-byte samples size), the host patches' behaviour, and the song-end rule (stop routine run twice, or every voice's sequence wrapped, whichever first) with the host's end-of-song writes. Decisions where the specification leaves the choice: the early family's envelope arpeggio (a crash in the original) is ignored; a sequence or pattern that never gives a note is cut off after 1,000 steps as a stop byte; the end-of-song writes follow the ending tick, at 100 Hz the tick after it (agrees with the one reference that shows it and with the silence after a stop); intra-tick spacing is a fixed approximation | Steve Barrett (the Eagleplayer UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | 6 of 6 modules recognised as `Steve Barrett`, with the same layout and flags as the earlier per-build table (checked before it was removed); 7 of 7 perturbed variants (code moved, tables moved, loads re-ordered, variables exchanged) recognised and 36 of 36 of their UADE runs identical in order and value to the port's, 7 of 7 deliberately broken files refused. Ordered Paula writes (DMACON and AUDxLC/LEN/PER/VOL, load-time stop writes included, LC relative to the module start) against UADE's: 32 of 32 runs of 20 s (every subsong, with and without `-n`, 7 of them ending in a song end, including the end-of-song writes) identical in order and value, and 12 of 12 runs of the specification's synthetic modules (which exercise the portamento, slide, arpeggio and flick paths the songs do not); two reference runs repeated serially gave the same sequences. Times per write against the first write of its tick: 6 to 12% exact, the rest late by at most 128 colour clocks (18 for Street Fighter) or early by up to 2,800 (path-dependent costs of the original, not modelled). None of the 7,546 present of the corpus lists' 10,181 listed modules is claimed. No regression: 3 modules of each `ports/*.txt` list (164 files) give identical WAV hashes before and after |
| Ashley Hogg (`ash.*`; the 1991 single-file drivers of CJ in the USA, Nobby the Aardvark and Spike in Transylvania, and the 1993 drivers of Cosmic Spacehead and Fantastic Dizzy, which need the sample companion `smp.*`; 5 modules, 69 subsongs, 5 builds) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/ashleyhogg.rs`; the first row of the 1993 period table is written out from the specification's). The module is a raw memory image: the driver code is never run, only read to recognise the family (four branches in the header, then the register save and the call that loads the chips' address for 1993, or the word move of 0 and the short branch over 34 bytes for 1991) and to find the tables, by the instruction forms the specification describes, matched on decoded instructions (`m68kdec`); the three 1991 builds (fade command, loop target, fourth voice only) are told apart the same way. 1991: four voices with a 74-byte state record, instruments copied over it, release/attack envelopes, arpeggios, sequence lists pointing at patterns of rows, a fade and the sequence-loop song end. 1993: pattern streams of note, effect and back-reference bytes, and a start sequence in three stages (DMA off, locations and DMA on, loop locations) with a nine-raster-line wait between the stages. 50 Hz ticks of 70,940 colour clocks. Decisions where the specification leaves the choice: subsongs numbered from 0; the start-up writes are made by the first tick; the song end is a flag that stays set and the player carries on; the 1991 attack follows the driver's own order of the delay counter; the unexercised synthetic-wave path points Paula at the module's own bytes; data-following loops are bounded; reads past the file give 0; the 1993 module is refused when its `smp.` companion is missing | Ashley Hogg (the Eagleplayer UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | 5 of 5 modules recognised as `Ashley Hogg`. Ordered Paula writes (DMACON and AUDxLC/LEN/PER/VOL, start-up included, LC relative to the first) against UADE's: 138 of 138 runs (every subsong, 20 s, with and without `-n`) and 4 of 4 long runs identical in order and value, the song end on the specified tick in all 9 runs that reach it inside the log; two reference runs repeated gave the same sequences. Times per write against the first write of its tick: 1991 mean 186, median 120, 95th percentile 488, max 1,762 colour clocks (279,646 writes); 1993 mean 190, median 145, 95th 458, max 1,231 (1,067,776 writes). None of the 7,546 present of the corpus lists' 10,181 listed modules is claimed. No regression: 3 modules of each `ports/*.txt` list (164 files) give identical WAV hashes before and after |
| Core Design (`core.*`; Action Fighter, Dynamite Dux, Monty Python's Flying Circus, Rick Dangerous, Saint and Greavsie, shared sound effects) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/coredesign.rs`, with the 68000 instruction decoder `src/replay/m68kdec.rs`). A file is an AmigaDOS load file with one chip code hunk that holds the driver's own copy of the replay code, its tables, songs and samples; the code is not run. The tables are found by reading the driver's code with the decoder: the subsong table and the song-init, output and sequencer routines from the subsong-start and play routines' address loads and calls, the sequence, pattern, period, vibrato and sample tables from the program-counter-relative loads of the song-init and sequencer routines, the note offset from the one add-immediate-to-byte of the sequencer, the silent block from the long immediate the output and interrupt routines store, the code family from the interrupt routine's shift (family B) or the output routine's flag-bit test (6 or 1), and the effect rules from the shape of the subsong-start routine; a module where any of that fails is refused. The engine is written from the behaviour: three music tracks on channels 1 to 3 (channel 0 for effects) with sequences, patterns, vibrato tables and a four-stage envelope; a note start switches DMA and its interrupt off, and the next output step restarts DMA; the audio interrupt routine (which Paula raises through the repo's model, at the first line after the DMA-on or at once if pending) loads the next block and acknowledges with INTENA then INTREQ. Decisions where the specification leaves the choice: the host's volume scaling is not applied (the defaults are the identity); the writes are spaced by the specification's median gaps and the interrupt routine's entry, per-channel scan and work are costed from its table (the order of the writes depends on it only where an interrupt falls near a write); reads outside the image give 0; the stop path (repeat 0, never taken with the host) stops the four channels. Known differences: in Dynamite_Dux subsongs 1, 3 and 8 (6 runs) one acknowledge pair of another channel's pending request is taken in the port in a tick where the reference's handler did not see it (the request's timing against the handler's read is within the specification's open question 2), and in 24 further runs some ticks carry the same writes in a different order (interrupt acknowledges against the output step); both are timing, not values | Core Design (the Eagleplayer UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | 6 of 6 corpus modules recognised as `Core Design`. Ordered Paula writes against UADE's for 20 s (plain and `-n`), tick by tick, start-up writes and interrupt acknowledges included, sample addresses relative: 254 of 290 runs identical in order and value; 30 more identical per channel (the same writes and values, some ticks with acknowledges in another order, for example Rick_Dangerous 0, 4 and Dynamite_Dux 7 of the ten long-period runs); 6 differ as stated above (Dynamite_Dux 1, 3, 8, which includes Dynamite_Dux 1 of the long-period runs). Write times against the reference's tick anchor: median error 1, 90th percentile 14, 99th percentile 106 colour clocks. None of the 7,546 present of the corpus lists' 10,181 listed modules is claimed. No regression: 3 modules of each `ports/*.txt` list (164 files) give identical WAV hashes before and after |
| David Hanney (`dh.*`; Tearaway Thomas, 8 modules) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original player code (`src/replay/davidhanney.rs`). A module is a chunk container (`DSNG`, `SEQU`, `INFO`, `BLK`+n) with only the order and the patterns; four voices, one tick a video frame, synth instruments with envelopes, vibrato and a pulse sweep that rewrites the looped wave, three PCM instruments, effects portamento, envelope speed, arpeggio and retrigger. The 16 instrument records and the three PCM samples are not in a module: they are taken from the original player (David Hanney, 1992) and embedded (`src/replay/davidhanney_data.bin`) on the project owner's decision. The writes are spaced by the specification's integer-cycle timing model. | David Hanney (EP 2.06 / UADE), with its write log | 8 of 8 real modules and 6 of 6 synthetic modules give the same ordered Paula writes at the same colour clocks as the 68k player (period table entries 43 and 69.. differ from the specification text: 564 for index 43, and past index 68 the words that follow the reference's table, which are the arpeggio table); audio SIMILAR (differences of about 0.7 percent of full scale, sub-sample phase) |
| Fred Gray (`gray.*`; Black Lamp, Star Goose, Victory Road, Eco, 1988-90) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original player code (`src/replay/fredgray.rs`). The files are hunk executables that carry the game's own sound driver (play and init routines named in a header, section routines and tables in the one code hunk); that code is run, on the repo's 68000 core with the standard cycle table, like Dave Lowe's. The hunk is relocated into a memory of its own; what the Eagleplayer adds is reproduced from the specification: section 0 of the jump table (found as the run of relocated longs whose first entry names a `rts`) reports the song end, and the glide decision of the note start (status register saved around a `bclr #7`, and the branch on it) is overwritten with no-ops. The wrapper's time at each store to an audio register of the play routine is a table of 68000 cycles (two builds: stores through `a5`, and absolute stores, Eco), as are its time before the play routine and at the song end, and the time before the first init is a table for the four known modules (the specification does not give it; another module gets about the mean) | Fred Gray (UADE), with its write log | 20 of 20 subsongs of the 4 modules give the same ordered Paula writes as the 68k player at the same half colour clocks relative to the song start, 60 s (reference logs that carry the 68000 cycle), song ends on the same tick (Black Lamp 3, Victory Road 2, 5, 6, 7). Audio: Black Lamp, Star Goose, Victory Road and Eco IDENTICAL, every subsong. The library builds without `host-68k` |
| Mark Cooksey (`mc.*`; Commando, Paperboy, Ghosts 'n Goblins, Grand National, Gremlins 2, Mighty Bomb Jack, World Championship Soccer; and the pair `mcr.*` + `mcs.*`, code and tables beside song data and samples; Aquablast, Live and Let Die, Mike Reads Comp Pop Quiz, Overlander, A Question of Sport; 14 modules) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/markcooksey.rs`). The module's code is never run: its tables are found from the position-independent address loads of the play routine (the specification's table-location rule, with its layout checks) and read as data. Three engines: N-A and N-B (one file, 1989-1990; B has a hardware-initialisation call and a different set of code costs) with the track commands, instrument records (envelope, pitch table, portamento) and period table of the module, and O (1988; the `mcs.NAME` companion beside the `mcr.NAME` is read from the same directory, the 28-byte header skipped) with its pattern and instrument tables and the default period table of the `mcr` file. A note start follows the original's sequence: raster-line waits, period 1, stop and the wait for the channel's audio interrupt flag (polled on the Paula model), new sample, start, wait, loop part (N); two waits of nine raster lines (O). The code between writes is charged per path in colour clocks, fitted to the reference's write logs. 50 Hz ticks of 70,940 colour clocks. Decisions where the specification leaves the choice: subsongs numbered from 0; the song end is reported when every voice that has a track has stopped or jumped; unknown track commands stop the voice; pattern nesting deeper than 3 replaces the innermost frame; the reference's end-of-song tail (a final group of volume writes and its stop a few ticks after the report) is not made | Mark Cooksey (the Eagleplayer UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | Ordered Paula writes (all audio registers, LC relative to the module) against the reference's for the 13 logged runs (Commando 0 and 1, Paperboy, Grand National title and ingame, Ghosts 'n Goblins 1, Mighty Bomb Jack 0 and 10, Gremlins 2, World Championship Soccer, Aquablast, Overlander, A Question of Sport; up to 1000 ticks each): every tick identical, except the last 3 of Mighty Bomb Jack 10 (the reference's end-of-song tail). Write times are approximate: the note-start sequences follow the specified waits (most runs within 50 colour clocks of the reference over a tick), the other gaps are fitted costs (median error under 1 colour clock on the fitted gaps, tails of 10 to 100) |
| Leggless Music Editor (`lme.*`; Punisher, Ninja Spirit with 17 subsongs, and its unused demo) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/legglessmusiceditor.rs`; the 37-word period table is the specification's). The module holds everything (instrument table, track lists, patterns, samples). Four voices, one Paula channel each, 50 Hz: events of a note or rest with instrument select, pitch table (arpeggio), slide or tie; looped 8-bit samples or a synth waveform of two levels whose switch point moves (the 128-byte buffers per voice are allocated by the player); attack / hold / release envelope, vibrato with delay, sample retrigger. Every register write is placed at the cycle of the specification's cost model (stage and event costs, the wait for nine raster-line changes before the closing DMACON, the song-end host call with its half-colour-clock re-basing); the first tick's entry time and the host's stop groups of a subsong change are the reference host's measured ones. The song ends when all four voices have wrapped their track lists | Leggless Music Editor (the Eagleplayer UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | Value, order and absolute colour-clock time of every Paula register write equal the reference's in all 19 logged runs (Punisher game_over, Ninja Spirit 17 subsongs, Ninja Spirit Demo subsong 5; 20 s each), checked by `tools/replay-cmp.py` and by an absolute comparison; refcmp IDENTICAL for the 3 corpus modules |
| Sound Programming Language (`spl.*`, Holger Gehrmann 1987-88: Boulder Dash, Clever & Smart, Graffiti Man with 8 subsongs, Hollywood Poker with 2) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/soundprogramminglanguage.rs`). A module is an Amiga executable that embeds its whole sound driver, tables, note streams and samples behind the Wanted Team's header (`!SOPROL!`, entry points, subsong count, title, timer); the code is never run, the driver's behaviour is written here from the specification and works on a mutable copy of the code hunk (the driver's own memory). Three driver generations (family 1 Boulder Dash: 0x3A-byte channel records, arpeggio/decay/envelope effects, 8-tick swing tempo; family 2 Clever & Smart and Graffiti Man: sample tables, tempo mask, song select, stop and transposition commands; family 3 Hollywood Poker: 16-byte records, a parser step every tick), one note stream language (duration, call, return, jump, instrument load), the same note and period maths, and a note-on that restarts the voice behind a busy-wait the host turned into a raster wait. A module is recognised by the header and by one of the four known layouts (the offsets of the driver's tables and variables, checked against the period table, timer value, global transposition and tables in the module's data); a module of another layout is left to the 68k player. Clever & Smart's call stack overflows into its sample length table; the pushed values are BASE + position, so some AUDxLEN values depend on the load address, and the port reproduces the reference run's load address (0x16750) for them. Write times: the specification's raster wait (first beam read 41 colour clocks after the write, then 8, then every 12.5; the wait ends in the ninth line) with per-module costs of the stream commands, effects and note-on gaps fitted by regression to the reference logs | Sound Programming Language (UADE's Eagleplayer), run with its write log | Value and order of every Paula write equal the reference's in all 12 logged runs (Boulder Dash, Clever & Smart, Graffiti Man songs 1 to 8, Hollywood Poker songs 1 and 2: 25 s each, 4,641 ticks that write) and in the two synthetic envelope modules; tick-relative times mostly within a few colour clocks (about 5% of the writes after a raster wait differ by one line, 227 colour clocks), audio SIMILAR in refcmp for all four modules |

The module is the game's sound driver (position-independent 68k code, never run) followed by its data; the song table, period table and subsong count are found by the specification's scan of the driver's address loads, and detection follows its pattern rule. Four voices, one Paula channel each, a note stream per voice (notes with duration, rest, jump, call/return, loop, instrument-table select, volume, pitch effect, voice end), a negative speed value skipping every n-th tick, the song table's own timer. Every register write is placed at the clock of the original's code (static 68000 cycle costs of the tick, the commands and the note start, whose two raster-line waits use the absolute colour clock of the tick call). Choices where the reference is undefined (a positive speed value, stream commands 10 to 13, a zero pitch-effect divisor, a stack past 128 bytes, a stream leaving the file): the voice ends, a positive speed plays one tick and ends the song | Paul Summers (the Eagleplayer UADE (`uade123`) ships), run with its write log | Value, order and absolute colour clock (relative to the tick call) of every register write equal the reference's in all 23 logged subsong runs of the 4 corpus modules (about 204,700 tick writes) and in the 5 synthetic edited modules' runs (pitch effect, loops, deep call stacks, rests, zero durations, song end in every voice order, positive and negative speed), after the host's start burst |

A module is one raw signed 8-bit sample, each big-endian long exclusive-or'ed with 0xAA55FF00, played on all four channels at period 222 (volumes 64, 64, 1, 1) from two alternating 1024-byte buffers; the audio-0 interrupt decodes the next 1024 bytes into the idle buffer and repoints the channels, the last buffer is zero-padded, the song end is reported and the sample restarts. Detection is by content only (the first 100 bytes, the player's own rule); files under 1024 bytes are not taken (the original misbehaves). Detection by the magic alone (length under 16 refused), never the file name, which another format shares. Lengths under 1800 and truncated files are handled safely (unspecified); the stop writes are from the player's description only, unobserved | NTSP-system (the Eagleplayer UADE ships), run with its write log | No real module exists in the archive; on the 22 synthetic reference modules the ordered register writes, the start-up times and every interrupt's write spacing equal the reference's (0 to 2 colour clocks), the interrupt entry times come from the engine's Paula, and the audio is IDENTICAL on the three modules rendered |

Where the reference is undefined (a position of 512 or more counts as the song's end; a missing 0xFFFF terminator scans all 2048 words; a track past the file's end is empty rows) the voice goes on safely; the silent-sample block is a zero block the replayer allocates itself | UADE's Eagleplayer "SpeedyA1System" (EP206 has none), run with its write log | Value, order and absolute colour clock of every Paula write equal the reference's in all 14 logged runs (the corpus module in full, 472,896 writes, and 13 synthetic modules), apart from the silent-sample address; audio IDENTICAL on the 7 files compared |
| Quartet (`qpa.*` with a companion `SMP.*` or `SMP.set`; Dan Lennard 1990; the Atari ST variants are different formats, not covered) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/quartet.rs`). A module is a tempo byte, the mark 0x50, rows of four 2-byte channel entries (volume, octave, sample, note) and an end mark of four words 0xFFFF; the 8-bit samples come from the companion set (checked by its `lock` text and exact size). Four voices, no envelope, a portamento that runs from a note to the next note of the channel, song end at the end mark (restart for songs over two 32-row blocks). Detection is by the tempo byte, the mark and the end mark with at most 15 zero words behind it. Every write is placed at the cycle count of the specification's ledger, including the two nine-line raster waits of each note start (modelled from the beam phase) and the start-up calls' cost for the first tick, which uses the real directory and file name lengths. Where the reference is undefined (a slide search past the file's end reads zero; the `SMP.set` fallback costs the same start-up time; host volume, balance and voice masks are not modelled; the zero buffer of one-shot samples is allocated by the replayer) the voice goes on safely. Only synthetic modules exist (no real Quartet module was found) | UADE's Eagleplayer "Quartet" (EP206 has it too), run with its write log | Value, order and absolute colour clock of every Paula write equal the reference's in all 36 logged runs (6 hand-made and 30 random synthetic modules, 48,712 writes), apart from the sample-set addresses; audio IDENTICAL on all 36 files |
| Tomy Tracker (`sg.*`; Tom Pakarinen 1992; no real module known) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code, nothing embedded (`src/replay/tomytracker.rs`). A module is a 704-byte song header (total and song size, 31 sample records, restart, length, position list), 64-row patterns of four 4-byte cells (command times 4, parameter, instrument times 7, note times 2) and the 8-bit samples. Four voices, a volume per sample, tone portamento, period slides, vibrato, volume slide, arpeggio, pattern break, filter and speed commands. Detection is the specification's strict content rule (sizes, song size on a pattern boundary, length 1..127, largest position entry equal to the last pattern) and the replayer is registered last. Every write is placed at the cycle count of the specification's ledger, including the two nine-line raster waits of each row tick (modelled from the beam phase) and the start-up calls' cost for the first tick. Where the reference is undefined (command byte not 4 times 0..13, instrument byte not a multiple of 7 or unset, note above 36, restart 0 or beyond the length, set speed 0, host volume/balance/mask) the voice goes on safely. Only synthetic modules exist | UADE's Eagleplayer "Tomy Tracker" (EP206 has none), run with its write log | Value, order and absolute colour clock of every Paula and CIA write equal the reference's in all 12 logged synthetic runs (58,423 writes), apart from the sample addresses (module-relative equal); audio IDENTICAL on all 12 files |
| MIDI - Loriciel (`MIDI.NAME` song with `SMPL.NAME` bank, or `NAME.mid` with `NAME.BSP`; the intro music of Loriciel's Entity, 1993; no real module known) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code, nothing embedded (`src/replay/midiloriciel.rs`). The song is a Standard MIDI File (format 0 or 1, no running status; note, program, tempo, channel-prefix and end-of-track events are used, the rest is skipped) and the bank (magic `BNKS`) holds programs of key regions that point at signed 8-bit one-shot samples. Four voices: a note takes the first free voice or steals one, a note-off frees only the channel's latest voice, and every tick each owned voice is pointed at a four-byte silence block so the sample plays once and then loops on silence. Detection is the song header's strict rule and the pair must load, so the replayer is registered last. Every write is placed at the cycle count of the specification's ledger, including the nine-line raster wait after each DMA write (modelled from the beam phase), the timer latch that follows a tempo event one tick late and the start-up calls' cost for the first tick. Where the reference is undefined (program change to a missing program, note outside the period table or above every region, tempo with a zero divisor, track running off the file, bank with an empty program or a reference outside it, host volume/balance/mute) the voice goes on safely. The two noise buffers are generated by the specification's generator. Every cycle count is the specification's ledger. YM envelopes are lost in both modes, as in the original. | ADPCM mono (Eagleplayer "ADPCM_mono", Adaptative Delta Pulse Code Modulation; file names ending in `.ADPCM`, case rule of the original: `x.adpcm` in all lower case is refused; headerless raw 4-bit ADPCM; no real module known) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/adpcmmono.rs`; the 89 step values are the standard IMA table). Each byte is two codes, high nibble first, decoded to signed 8-bit samples (the high byte of the 16-bit predictor) 1024 at a time into two chip buffers that all four channels play (volumes 64, 64, 1, 1; period 125); the audio channel 0 interrupt, once a buffer, fills the other one and points the channels at it. The stream ends in a zero-padded (or, for a multiple of 1024 samples, silent) buffer, the song end is announced and the stream restarts. Write moments follow the original's decode cost (saturations and index clamps). `detect` sees no file name and accepts any content but the `ADPC` magic and what libxmp or the built-in ProTracker take, so the format is the last of the open replayers and the name is checked when it starts. A file shorter than 512 bytes plays as one zero-padded buffer (the original decodes memory past it). The format name carries no `adpcm`, so the output stage is the Amiga one (A500 filter), as the original's run | ADPCM_mono (UADE `uade123`'s Eagleplayer), run with its write log | Synthetic modules only (the corpus has no `.ADPCM` file). Ordered Paula writes and values equal to the original's on all 8 reference modules (times within 1-3 colour clocks after the host's start phase, which varies per module), audio IDENTICAL to the original (6 s, tools/refcmp.py) on 7 of them; the all-zero module's original is silent in the engine (NOREF). `tiny.ADPCM` (300 bytes) is not reproduced past its data |
| ADPCM2 / ADPCM3 (Eagleplayer "ADPCM", ADPCM routines by Christian Buchner; files starting `ADPCM2` or `ADPCM3` and a big-endian sample rate, mono 2-bit or 3-bit ZyXEL-style voice-modem ADPCM; three real files known: Dancing Moon, the two halves of Para Mi Amiga) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/adpcm23.rs`; the six step multipliers are typed in from the specification). A 10-byte header, then the codes to the end of the file (ADPCM2: 4 a byte, top pair first; ADPCM3: 8 in 3 bytes, a bit stream); the step is multiplied by one of six fixed factors after each code, the 16-bit predictor wraps, the output is its bits 13-6 clamped to a signed byte. Played as the original's amplifier interface asks: both Paula channels 0 and 1 at volume 64 and period `5 * E / rate`, two 1024-byte chip buffers (the first, silent), the audio channel 0 interrupt refills the other one once per 1024-sample block, the partial last block is zero-padded (up to two bytes of the tail are never played), the song end is announced and the stream restarts with a fresh decoder state. Recognition is strict (`ADPCM2` / `ADPCM3` and ten header bytes), so it never meets the headerless ADPCM mono format, which refuses `ADPC`. The decode cost of the original's 68000 is not modelled (a stream above about 20-25 kHz makes the original repeat buffers). The format name carries `adpcm`, so the output stage is the PC one (no Amiga filters), as the original's run. The stock host plays this Eagleplayer silently (nothing calls its refill), so there is no audio reference | ADPCM (Eagleplayer 0.85) run through the host extended with the one missing call (the player's refill on channel 0's audio interrupt), write and DMA logs | The three real files and 9 synthetic ones. Played samples (both channels) equal to the original's DMA words on all 12 over the logged span (5 s for the real files, 2 s for the synthetic, restarts included; the overloaded synthetic 22 050 Hz file to its overload), and the whole real files equal an independent decode to the song end. Ordered Paula writes and values equal to the original's on all (complete to the song end for the real files); clocks equal on refills after the first (the first refill and the start phase vary by module in the original's host: up to 11 colour clocks, a raster line on the real files). No audio reference (NOREF) |

A ProTracker-style four-channel player with packed patterns (literal runs and back references unpacked at each position change), sample slots resolved by name from the set (aliases, delta coding, repeat fix-ups), a slide mask that starts at zero, the tremolo's extra phase step, a glissando that walks off the period table and reads what lies around it. Every write is placed from the spec's fitted cost table, with the raster waits of its output phase. The reference's start-up time (loading of the files) is a constant. Only synthetic modules exist | UADE's Eagleplayer "Nick_Pelling_Packer", run with its write log | Value and order of every Paula write equal the reference's on the 20 synthetic modules; colour-clock times 78% exact, 99.1% within 1 (the costs are a fit; the rest are raster-wait polls); audio SIMILAR on all 20 |
| Voodoo Supreme Synthesizer (`vss.*`, extension form `NAME.vss`; Tomas Partl, Voodoo Software 1993; the Eagleplayer is named "VSS") | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/voodoosupremesynthesizer.rs`). A module is data only: a `VSS0` header at the end of the file (subsong count, one song-table offset per subsong), a table of object offsets and the objects (four note streams, waveforms, volume and pitch envelopes, wave sequences, samples). Four voices; per video frame a note stream (notes, rests, calls, loops, portamento, transpose, jumps), a volume and a pitch envelope, a pitch command (add, subtract, double, halve, transpose by semitones with a ratio table) and one of four synth/sample modes: a mix of two waves, an xor window, a morph window (the first three double-buffered 32-byte halves handed to Paula by AUDxLC), or a sample played block by block by the audio interrupt handler. The two player tables (96 note periods, 13 semitone ratio pairs) are embedded (`voodoosupremesynthesizer_period.bin`, `_ratio.bin`) on the project owner's decision; nothing else of the original is. Every write is placed by the specification's cycle ledger; the host calls the tick once a frame, delayed by a running interrupt handler, and the handler is the port's (acknowledge, and the sample chain). Where the reference is undefined (an undefined command byte, a portamento of length 0 or between equal notes, a note index of 122 or more, nesting past 20 stack entries, a return or loop end with nothing stacked, a read that never ends) the voice falls silent and the others go on; a pitch-corrected sample step against a reference period past the tables plays the plain step; a module whose song-table offsets lie outside the file is not recognised. Two corpus modules have a voice that never selects a pitch envelope and reads the machine's memory from address 0: the host's bytes there are reproduced | UADE's Eagleplayer "VSS" (EP206's build plays silent in the engine), run with its write log | Value, order and absolute colour clock of every Paula write equal the reference's in all 13 logged runs (7 corpus modules in full, subsongs 1-3 of the 4-subsong one through a header edit, 3 synthetic modules; 2.2 million writes, including the audio-interrupt acknowledgements and sample-chain writes), the synth-buffer addresses included; audio IDENTICAL on the 7 corpus files |
| AProSys (`aps.*`; the Eagleplayer is named "AProSys"; no real module is known, all test modules are synthetic) | Clean-room, written from a behavioural specification and black-box runs; no original code (`src/replay/aprosys.rs`). Nothing of the original is embedded: the 48 note periods are constants. An `ADRVPACK` file of nine length-prefixed chunks (sequence, 26-byte blocks, track rows, arpeggio bytes, 26-byte instruments, envelope points, 6-byte event records, sample data). Four voices at 50 Hz; per tick arpeggio or portamento, four envelopes, pitch and volume routing and the event action: looped or one-shot samples and sample chains played by per-channel audio interrupt handlers, and a synth built into 128-byte chip buffers (generators, mixers, comb and filter stages) handed to Paula double-buffered. Register writes are placed on the clock by timings fitted to the reference logs. The original never reports the song end (it writes INTENA 0x0780 and DMACON 0x000F once and plays on); the port reports it at that write and the engine stops. Undefined cases (periods outside the table, envelope numbers past voice 3, a sequence that never ends) are bounded and silent, no panic | UADE's and EP206's AProSys, run with their write logs | Value and order of every register write equal the reference's up to the port's song end on 8 of 17 logged runs (r1mix, r1synth, r2mix, r2synth, r3mix, r3synth, g16, g18); the other 9 diverge at the first audio-DMA interrupt whose firing depends on a few colour clocks of Paula word phase (very long periods). Median write time error under about 150 colour clocks. refcmp (original taken to play on after the song end): SIMILAR on 12 of 17, DIFF r1sample, r2synth, g15, LEVEL r2mix, FILTER g13 |

A module is a plain 68k image with its own driver; the replayer never runs it but locates the module's tables from marker words (record size, sample table and data, period table, record table, song table) and interprets them. Three variants (A, B, C with a period slide); four voices, a row every second 50 Hz call and a DMA stop call in between, start-up writes and timing from the specification's measured values (the host's detection scan cost included). Choices where the reference is undefined: the link origin is the specification's rule, corrected by the streams' contiguity when the first data after the list is unreferenced; period and arpeggio reads past a table read the relocated memory image; a missing list terminator or out-of-file reads give zero; the song end is the list's wrap | UADE's Eagleplayer "SUN-Tronic" (the EP206 build "SUNtronic" does not report the song end), run with its write log | Value, order and clock of every Paula write equal the reference's on the two real modules (3074 and 1999 ticks) and on 9 of 10 synthetic runs (one synthetic module is undefined from tick 0); two wrap ticks are 1 colour clock off; Paula DMA content equal to the reference except 6 of 164,129 words on one module |
